The paraphrase of Rabbi *Jonathan* is, “To him who offers to them a feast of meat, they prophesy peace.” *Jerome’s* view is the same. *Dathias* gives this paraphrase, *dum illis datur quod edunt—*”While is given them something to eat.” *Henry’s* comment is much to the purpose,—”They will flatter and compliment those who will feed them with good bits, will give them something to eat; but as for those who put not into their mouths, who are not continually cramming them, they look upon them as their enemies; to them they do not cry, Peace, but even prepare war against them; against them they denounce the judgments of God: they preach either comfort or terror to men, not according as they are to God, but as they are to them; as the crafty priests of the Church of Rome, in some places, make their image either to smile or to frown upon the offerer, according as his offering is.”—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 254 -->

[^f44]: The original may be thus rendered,— <span style="color:#000080">*But whosoever will not put into their mouth,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Even against him they will proclaim war.—*</span><span style="color:#000080">Ed.</span>

[^f45]: That this is the meaning is evident from the two last lines of the verse. It is a kind of parallelism, in which the four lines contain the same idea or ideas, announced in the two first lines in one form, and in the two last another, with more clearness, and sometimes with something additional. The preposition <span style="color:#008080">m</span> has sometimes the meaning of *rather than,* but here, *instead of.* I would render the verse thus,— <span style="color:#000080">*Therefore night shall be to you instead of vision,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And darkness shall be to you instead of divination:*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Yea, set shall the sun upon the Prophets,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And darken upon them shall the day.*</span> *Piscator* gives the sense when he says, *Visio vestra mutabitur in noctem*—”Your vision shall be changed into night.”—*Ed.*

[^f46]: <span style="color:#008080">µymsr</span>, <span style="color:#0000d4">manteiv</span>, Sept. *fatidici,* sootsayers, diviners. It is used generally in a bad sense, while <span style="color:#008080">µyzj</span>, seers is commonly used in a good sense; but here both words denote pretenders, the false prophets.—*Ed.*

[^f47]: The version of Newcome is, “They shall cover the mouth;” that is, as he adds in a note, “with part of the long eastern vesture. This action was a sign of being put to silence, of disgrace and dejection, Leviticus 13:45; Ezekiel 24:17, 22.” There is no reason to render mouth, beard, as some have done.—*Ed.*

[^f48]: *Jerome* renders “the Spirit of Jehovah” in the genitive case, which in meaning amounts to the same thing; but *Newcome* puts the words in apposition with “power.” The <span style="color:#008080">ta</span> before Spirit seems to betoken a difference in its connection with “filled.” It appears to be here a preposition, *ab,* by. The “power,” <span style="color:#008080">jk</span>, is the <span style="color:#0000d4">dunamiv</span> of the New Testament; and “judgment,” <span style="color:#008080">fpçm</span>, is discernment or discrimination; and “might” or courage, <span style="color:#008080">hrwbg</span>, is “an intrepid firmness of mind,” as *Marckius* observes, “against all opposing evils and hindrances.”—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 255 -->

[^f49]: *Marckius* renders it the same, *verè,* and says, that it is of the same import with *Amen, Amen,* so often used by our Savior. *Truly* is adopted by *Newcome* and *Henderson.*—*Ed.*

[^f50]: *Scelus,* <span style="color:#008080">[çp</span>; it means defection, apostacy, rebellion, a willful transgression, and a proud contempt of divine law and institutions; it is <span style="color:#0000d4">anomia</span>—lawlessness, as it is sometimes rendered by the Septuagint. But “sin,” <span style="color:#008080">hafj</span>, is a deviation from what is right through delusion, mistaken views, error, ignorance, or infirmity. The first included idolatry and gross acts of imposture and oppression; the second, the superstition of the people, and their common vices. *Muis,* as quoted by *Leigh,* says that <span style="color:#008080">[çp</span> is “defection from God or rebellion, and prevarication towards God,”—*defectio à Deo seu rebellio, ac praevaricatio in Deum.* And he quotes also *Mollerius* as describing <span style="color:#008080">hafj</span> as including not only sins of error, ignorance, and infirmity, but also those of omission, —*Ea potissimum peccata significat, quae vel errore, vel per ignorantiam, vel per infirmitatem carnis, committuntur; item peccata omissionis.—Ed.*

[^f51]: It is <span style="color:#008080">hnb</span>, in the singular number, with no variation as to number in the MSS. The Septuagint gives it a plural participle, <span style="color:#0000d4">oiJoikodomountev</span>. It may be rendered as a participial noun, “The building of Zion *is* by blood,” etc., for <span style="color:#008080">h</span>, when radical, does not always turn into <span style="color:#008080">t</span>, when in *regimine.—Ed.*

[^f52]: It often happens, as in the present case, that the relative <span style="color:#008080">h</span>, in Hebrew, prefixed to a participle, has after it a verb in the future connected by <span style="color:#008080">w</span>, and in person different from that to which the relative refers. The relative here refers to a noun in the second, and the verb connected with the participle is in the third person. It is an idiom, of which there are frequent instances. We find the same to be the case with the relative <span style="color:#008080">rça</span>, in the third verse. It refers to the chiefs, who are addressed, and must therefore be viewed as in the second person, and all the verbs which follow it are in the third. Some render the participle, “who hate,” which is in Hiphil, in a causative sense. See Amos 5:7; 6:12. The distich may then be rendered thus,— <span style="color:#000080">*Who render judgment hateful, (or, abominable,)*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And distort everything that is right,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">or more literally,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And make crooked everything that is straight.*</span><span style="color:#000080">—Ed.</span>

<!-- PAGE 256 -->

[^f53]: “They pretend,” says *Henry,* “in justification of their extortion and oppressions, that they build up Zion and Jerusalem; they add new streets and squares to the holy cities and adorn them; they establish and advance the public interests both in church and state, and think therein they do God and Israel good service; but it is with blood and with iniquity, and therefore it cannot prosper; nor will their intentions of good to the city of God justify their contradictions to the law of God.” A flaming zeal for a good cause can never consecrate extortion, injustice, and murder. It may be asked, What is the difference between *Zion* and *Jerusalem?* Zion was the church, Jerusalem was the state; or it may be, that, according to the usual style of the Prophets, the more limited idea is given first, and the more extensive one is added to it.—*Ed.*

[^f54]: “As the *Masoretes,* in their division of the Bible, reckon the *Twelve Minor Prophets* but as *one* Book, they mark this verse (*twelfth* of chapter 3) the *middle* verse of these Prophets.”*—Adam Clarke.*

[^f55]: *Calvin* has *mercede* in both instances. The first in Hebrew is <span style="color:#008080">djç</span>, a gift, a bribe; this was given to the princes: and the second is <span style="color:#008080">ryjm</span>, a commutation, barter, price, something in exchange; this was given to the priests: and then what was given to the prophets is literally silver, <span style="color:#008080">ãsk</span>; but it often means money in general. The Septuagint renders the first, <span style="color:#0000d4">meta dwrwn</span>—for gifts; the second, <span style="color:#0000d4">meta misqou</span>—for reward; and the last, <span style="color:#0000d4">meta arguriou</span>—for money. The princes decided matters according to the bribes given them, the priests, not satisfied with the regular allowance given them according to the law, did not teach except they were paid, had something in exchange, a reward for their trouble. And while the true prophets, who were extraordinary teachers sent by God, delivered their messages freely, without any pay, as they received them; the false prophets, who pretended that they came from God, required money for performing their office; see Jeremiah 6:13. And notwithstanding all their gains, all things were done badly. Money was extracted for doing wrong. The princes determined cases unjustly, the priests taught erroneous doctrine, and the prophets prophesied falsely: and yet for all these evils, money was required! How ignorant and infatuated the people must have been! *Cocceius* enumerated *six* things as chargeable on the persons mentioned in this verse: 1. Avarice—the seeking of wealth instead of doing God’s will; 2. A mercenary disposition, influenced by gain and not by sense of duty; 3. The exacting of unlawful reward; 4. The doing, even for reward, of what was evil and wicked; 5. A false pretense of trust in God; and, 6. The tying of God’s favor to external privileges.*—Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 257 -->

[^f56]: In unison with the foregoing are these striking remarks of *Henry,—* ”Many are rocked sleep in a fatal security by their church privileges, as if these would protect them in sin and shelter them from punishment, which are really, and will be, the greatest aggravations both of their sin and of their punishment. If men’s having the Lord among them will not restrain them from doing evil, it can never secure them from suffering evil for so doing; and it is very absurd for sinners to think that their impudence will be their impunity.”—*Ed.*

[^f57]: That is, the promise from repentance.—*Ed.*

[^f58]: *In extremitate dierum,* <span style="color:#008080">µymyh tyrjab</span>, in the posteriority or postremity of the days; <span style="color:#0000d4">ep escatwn twn hmerwn</span>, in the last days.— *Sept.* “In the latter days,” or, “in the end of days.”—*Newcome.* “In the last of the days.”—*Henderson.* See Jeremiah 23:20; 30:24; Ezekiel 38:8; Daniel 10:14; Hosea 3:5. *Kimchi,* as quoted by *Lowth,* says, “Whenever the latter days are mentioned in Scripture, the days of the Messiah are always meant.”—*Ed.*

[^f59]: *Dispositus,* <span style="color:#008080">ˆzkn</span>—*constitus,* constituted—*praeparatus,* prepared— *firmatus,* made firm—are the words by which the term is commonly expressed. It comes from <span style="color:#008080">ˆwk</span>, which *Leigh* justly says, means “aptly and timely to frame, and likewise to make firm and sure;” and he adds, “The word noteth *the ordering, perfecting,* and *fast establishing* of anything.” How suitably then it is here used: it is a mountain (which means evidently the Church) that is fitly framed, ordered, and firmly established.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 258 -->

[^f60]: *Marckius* adduces the opinions of the ancients as to the signification of this “mount.” Some, such as *Tertullian, Jerome,* and *Augustine,* interpret it of Christ; while others, namely, *Origen,* the two *Cyrils,* and *Chrysostom,* regard it as signifying the Church; and with the latter most modern commentators agree. Here the consent of moderns exceeds that of the ancients; and it is no doubt sounder and wiser.—*Ed.*

[^f61]: *Convenient,* <span style="color:#008080">wrhnw</span>, literally, “and flow;” <span style="color:#0000d4">speusousi</span>—hasten, *Sept.* It is flowing like that of a river, or of a strong current, and implies copiousness and spontaneity. “There shall be,” says Henry, “a constant stream of believers flowing in from all parts into the Church, as the people of the Jews flowed into the temple, while it was standing, to worship there.” *Kimchi* says, that this word means to “run to what is pleasing or delightful,” —”*currere ad beneplacitum, hoc est, ad id quod cupias.* An old author, quoted by *Leigh,* says, that it implies abundance and celerity—*affluentiam cum celeritate.* It is rendered “flow together” in Jeremiah 51:44. Instead of “peoples,” <span style="color:#008080">µym[</span>, Isaiah has <span style="color:#008080">µywgj lk</span>, “all the nations.” One MS. Has the same here, and three have <span style="color:#008080">lk</span> before <span style="color:#008080">µym[</span>, and this seems to be the correct reading. <span style="color:#008080">µ[</span>, in the plural number, is synonymous with <span style="color:#008080">µywg</span>, meaning nations. The rest of this verse is exactly the same in the two Prophets, except that <span style="color:#008080">ˆwkn</span>, “prepared,” is differently placed, and <span style="color:#008080">awh</span>, “it,” is added by Micah after <span style="color:#008080">açn</span>, “exalted.” In the second verse, which is the third in Isaiah, there is a complete verbal identity, except that <span style="color:#008080">µywg</span> and <span style="color:#008080">µym[</span> are reversed, and that <span style="color:#008080">w</span> before <span style="color:#008080">la</span> is wanting in Isaiah; but it is supplied in several MSS. In the third, the fourth in Isaiah, there are verbal varieties in the two first lines, the four remaining are exactly the same with the exception of a paragogic <span style="color:#008080">ˆ</span>, *nun,* added to a verb by Micah, and the verb <span style="color:#008080">waçy</span> is singular in Isaiah. In the two lines referred to, there is also an addition of <span style="color:#008080">qwjr d[</span>, “afar of,” in Micah.

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">Isaiah. <span style="color:#008080">µywgh ˆyb fpçw</span> 4. <span style="color:#008080">µybr µym[ljykwhw</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 259 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">And he shall judge among the nation, And shall convince many peoples.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">Micah. <span style="color:#008080">µybr µym[ ˆyb fpçw</span> <span style="color:#008080">qwjr d[ µymx[ µywgl jykwhw</span> And he shall judge among many peoples, And shall convince strong nations afar off.</p>

With this verse the passage ends in Isaiah; Micah adds another: and this, with the two other circumstances—that the passage is fuller and more connected with the context here than in Isaiah, may seem to favor the opinion that Isaiah, and not Micah, was the copyist; but the words, with which the passage is introduced in Isaiah, forbid such a supposition.

“Bishop Lowth, on Isaiah 2:2, thinks that Micah took this passage from Isaiah. It is true that he has improved it after the manner of imitators. Or, the Spirit may have inspired both with this prediction: or both may have copied some common original, the words of a Prophet well known at the time.—*Newcome.*

[^f62]: *Marckius* says, “*corde, ore, et opere*—with the heart, mouth, and in deed.”

[^f63]: <span style="color:#008080">yk</span>, *ki,* for, or because: what follows contains the reason for the preceding promise. How could it be, that the mount of the house of Jehovah should be firmly fixed on the top of mountains, etc.? The answer is here given, “for go forth shall a law from Zion,” etc. And this was literally fulfilled at the commencement of the Gospel; it was first preached at Jerusalem: in consequence of this, the mount of Jehovah’s house, or the Church, was formed; and what is here predicted was in part fulfilled, and will no doubt be hereafter more completely fulfilled. It is said, “on top of the mountains,” not of a mountain. The Church was not to be confined to one place, but was to be preeminent throughout the earth. It was to be coextensive with the word that was to go forth from Zion.—*Ed.*

[^f64]: Both *Newcome* and *Henderson* render the <span style="color:#008080">w</span>, *vau,* here, *that,* “that he may teach us,” etc.: but it is better to retrain the most common meaning as a simple copulative, as it is done in our version, and by *Calvin,* and by *Lowth* in Isaiah. The passage thus runs better, and more emphatically expresses the language of faith.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 260 -->

[^f65]: So *Newcome* renders it; *Lowth* and *Henderson* read the same with our version, “the law.” The absence of the definite article is certainly no objection, as it is but seldom used in Hebrew. But “a law” is better except we render the copulative before “word,” even; and then we shall destroy the distinct character of the line. It appears that, according to the usual style of the Prophets, what the first line states indefinitely, is in the second specified, as being the “word of Jehovah.” The word <span style="color:#008080">hrwt</span>, law, in Hebrew, is more comprehensive than the word law, in our language. It is derived from the Hiphil of <span style="color:#008080">hry</span>, which means to direct, to appoint, to instruct, to teach. Hence the noun, <span style="color:#008080">hrwt</span>, may be rendered, directory, institution, law, teaching, or doctrine. “It doth,” says *Leigh*, “not only signify strictly what is to be done, but it denoteth largely any heavenly doctrine, whether it be promise or precept.” It means often the whole of God’s revealed word, as in Psalm 1:2; <span style="color:#800000"><19B9174></span>Psalm 119:174, etc. See Joshua 1:8.—*Ed.*

[^f66]: There is a difference of opinion as to the nominative case to the verb “judge;” whether it be Jehovah in the preceding line, or the word of Jehovah. The most natural construction is the last supposition. *Jerome* and *Cyril,* as quoted by *Marckius,* refer it to the word of Jehovah, taking the word for Christ: but this cannot be admitted, as the law and the word seem to mean the same thing, and must be considered as the word of the Gospel; and *Justin Martyr* and *Irenaeus,* when referring to this passage regard it as such. And this is the view which *Marckius* seems to prefer. The rendering then would be,— <span style="color:#000080">*And it shall judge among many people,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And convince strong nations afar off.*</span>

[^f67]: The two verbs here used are <span style="color:#008080">fpç</span>, to judge, and <span style="color:#008080">jykwh</span>, in Hiphil, to reprove. The first is to decide what is right and wrong, and also to defend the right and to punish the wrong; hence it means to arbitrate, and also to vindicate as well as to punish. The first sense is most suitable to this place.—The other verb does not occur in Kal, but in Hiphil, it means to make manifest, or show, by facts or by words, or by action; and hence it signifies to demonstrate, to convince, to reprove, to chastise. The Septuagint often renders it by <span style="color:#0000d4">elegcein</span>, which, Parkhurst says, means, in its primary sense, to demonstrate by convincing reasons or arguments. *Lowth’s* version in Isaiah is, “And shall work conviction,” etc. *Newcome* renders it “convince.” The rendering of *Henderson,* “give decision,” is not to be approved. See John 16:8.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 261 -->

[^f68]: “All these predictions must be confined to the nations converted by the word of Jehovah, and brought into Zion, that is, such as truly repent and believe, and must not be extended to all nations indiscriminately, or to all who embrace the Christian name, who are often as far as possible from the kingdom of Christ, inasmuch as they neither learn nor follow his doctrine.—*Marckius.*

[^f69]: *Marckius* views this passage differently. He considers that the converted Gentiles are meant here,—that when turned from their idols and their superstitions, they shall profess the true God, as revealed in the Gospel, and that each nation will regard him as its own God: however various in outward circumstances, they shall yet acknowledge the God revealed in his Word as their own. This view most certainly harmonizes better with the context than that of *Calvin,* which is commonly adopted. There is another, which is the same nearly in meaning, but founded on a different rendering of the words. The Jewish commentator *Abarbanel,* as quoted by *Marckius,* gives this version:— “Nam omnes populi, qui ambulabant quisque in nomine dei sui, et now ambulabimus in nomine Jehovae Dei nostri.” The words will no doubt admit of this construction; for it is often the case in Hebrew, that <span style="color:#008080">rça</span>, who, is understood before a verb in the future tense, especially when it has the meaning of the present, as here, for the preceding “ambulabant,” might be rendered “ambulant,” without any inconstancy in the meaning. I would therefore render the verse thus,— <span style="color:#000080">*For all the nations,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">Who</span> <span style="color:#000080">*walk each in the name of its god,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And we ourselves,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Shall walk in the name of Jehovah our God,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*For ever and ever.*</span> The nations were then walking in the name of their multiplied gods; but at the time alluded to, both Gentiles and Jews would walk together in the name of Jehovah. There is thus an entire correspondence between all the parts of this remarkable passage, which extends from the first verse to the seventh inclusive; a part of which, extending only to the end of the third verse, is to be found in Isaiah.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 262 -->

[^f70]: <span style="color:#008080">d[w µlw[l</span>, “for ages and perpetually.” <span style="color:#008080">µlw[</span> means most commonly an indefinite, rather than an infinite time. The verb signifies to be hidden or concealed; and so the noun means an undefined and unknown period. “For ages,” would perhaps be its best version; whether these ages be limited or unlimited must depend on the context. Here <span style="color:#008080">d[</span> is added to show that these ages would be endless, or to the end of time: for <span style="color:#008080">d[</span> is “still,” unceasing futurity, that which is perpetual, still the same. The Levitical dispensation was <span style="color:#008080">µlw[l</span> “for ages,” but the new state of things promised here is to be, not only for ages, but also perpetually, that is to the end of time, while the world lasts.—*Ed.*

[^f71]: It means, doubtless, no more here; some refer it to halting between two opinions, between idols and God: but such an idea is foreign to the drift of this passage. It is the depressed, weak, or afflicted and miserable state of the Church that is here set forth.—*Ed.*

[^f72]: It is a Niphal participle from <span style="color:#008080">alh</span>, and corresponds in meaning with <span style="color:#008080">hjdnh</span>, “the ejected,” in the last verse; only it is a stronger term, as it means one cast to a distance, while the latter signifies one cast or driven away. The first, as rendered by *Junius* and *Tremelius,* is *procul disjecta,* and the other is *depulsa.—Ed.*

[^f73]: “I think the temple is meant, or Jerusalem; the place where the *flock,* the whole *congregation* of the people assembled to worship God. *Newcome* retains the Hebrew word <span style="color:#008080">rd[</span>, *eder*, a tower in or near Bethlehem, Genesis 35:21, or as some think, a tower near the sheep gate in Jerusalem. I believe Jerusalem, or the temple, or both, are meant; for these were considered the *stronghold of the daughter of Zion,* the fortress of the Jewish people.”—*Adam Clarke.* What especially confirms this view is, that the two clauses are in apposition, the latter is explanatory of the former.—*Ed.*

[^f74]: *Calvin* observes the order of the original, which is not done in our version. The whole verse may be thus rendered,— <span style="color:#000080">*And thou tower of the flock,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*The fortress of the daughter of Zion!*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*To thee it shall return;*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Yea, come shall the former dominion,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*The kingdom to the daughter of Jerusalem.*</span> The verb <span style="color:#008080">hta</span>, which I render “return,” means mostly, to come, to come near, to approach, to happen.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 263 -->

[^f75]: *Tyne,* <span style="color:#008080">ht[</span>, is left out; nor is it restored in the comment. It should be, “For now thou shalt go forth from the city.”—*Ed.*

[^f76]: *Ingemisce,* groan, mourn, or sigh and sob. <span style="color:#008080">yjg</span>, burst forth, or break out; that is, into tears or mourning. “Bring forth,” as it is rendered by *Newcome* and *Henderson,* seems not to be the import of the word here. It may be rendered, as *Parkhurst* proposes, “labor and bring forth.”— *Ed.*

[^f77]: *Jam damnata erit. Newcome* renders the distich thus,— <span style="color:#000080">*Who say, Let her be defiled,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And let our eye see*</span> <span style="color:#000080">its desire</span> <span style="color:#000080">*on Zion.*</span> Profaned, or defiled, it is no doubt the meaning of the verb. But it is better to retain the future tense here, though it may often, in the third person, be rendered as an imperative. To look on, is a Hebrew idiom, and means often to triumph or exalt over another, or to gain the upper hand. See Psalm 22:17; <span style="color:#800000"><19B807></span>Psalm 118:7. Several copies have the word for “eyes” in the singular number, as the verb is so: but anomalies of this kind often occur, as it is the case in Greek with respect to plural nouns in the neuter gender, and in Welsh, and when the verb precedes its nominative, almost in all instances. I offer the following version,— <span style="color:#000080">*Who say, “Defiled shall she be,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And look on Zion shall our eyes.”—*</span><span style="color:#000080">Ed.</span>

[^f78]: The beginning of these two lines is very emphatic: I would give this rendering,— <span style="color:#000080">*But they—they know not the purposes of Jehovah,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And they understand not his counsel.*</span> It has been rendered, “But, as for them;” but this is flat, and too prosaic.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 264 -->

[^f79]: *Manipulum,* a handful, a bundle of fruit; <span style="color:#008080">rym</span>, a sheaf,—a poetical singular for the plural—sheaves.—*Ed.*

[^f80]: *Horn,* in Scripture, means often elevation, dignity, power, strength. It means evidently in the last here. Zion was made strong to thrash the nations, and supplied with strong hoofs to tread on them. The Paraphrase of Rabbi Jonathan is to the purpose, *Fortes sicut ferrum, et robusti sicut aes—*”Strong as iron, and robust as brass.” And that this is the meaning is proved by what follows, *Thou shalt beat in pieces,* or beat small, or thrash out, *strong nations.—Ed.*

[^f81]: It is not often that Calvin passes over a sentence without noticing it, but he does so here; and it is this, *and thou shalt tear in pieces strong nations.* The verb is <span style="color:#008080">twqdh</span>, thou shalt beat small, or thrash out; see Isaiah 28:28; perhaps the latter sense is most suitable to the passage. The meaning is, that a complete subjugation will take place. To thrash and to thrash out, is to conquer and to bring thoroughly under subjection.—*Ed.*

[^f82]: The Hebrew word for this is <span style="color:#008080">lyj</span>, and for “wealth” <span style="color:#008080">[xb</span>. The latter means gain, spoil, or what is often unjustly got, or what is scraped together and constitutes the wealth of the covetous; <span style="color:#008080">lyj</span> is properly substance, including possessions of all kinds, land, cattle, etc. <span style="color:#008080">[xb</span> serves to include money, silver and gold; and <span style="color:#008080">lyj</span>, everything else which makes up wealth. The verb, “consecrate,” is in Hebrew in the first person, as it is in our version. There is no different reading; but the Septuagint and the earlier versions put it in the second person, to correspond with the previous verb, “Thou shalt beat in pieces.” There will be no difference in the sense, if we render it according to the Hiphil form, in which it is found,—”I will cause thee to consecrate.” *Jerome, Theodoret, Marckius, Dathius, Newcome,* and *Henderson,* adopt the second person.—This construction renders the passage no doubt more uniform.—*Ed.*

[^f83]: The word is very emphatic; it means to devote a thing to a purpose forever, so as to be unchangeably settled. <span style="color:#008080">µrj</span>, says *Parkhurst,* “is anything *separated* absolutely from its common condition and *devoted* to Jehovah, so as to be incapable of redemption. See Leviticus 27:21, 28, 29. As a verb in Hiph. *To separate* or *devote* thus to Jehovah. Leviticus 27:28, 29; Micah 4:12.” It is therefore a sacrilege to take merely to our own use what ought to be, or what we have, thus consecrated to the Lord.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 265 -->

[^f84]: *Calvin* has, in this division, followed the Septuagint, and so have the translators of our version. This verse, in Hebrew, belongs to the last chapter. *Marckius, Dathius,* and *Henderson,* follow the Hebrew division; *Junius,* and *Tremelius,* and *Newcome,* that of our version.

[^f85]: This verse has been variously interpreted. It is considered by most as connected with the last chapter. Some, as *Marckius*, consider it as an address to the Roman power; some, to the Babylonian; and others, to Jerusalem. The construction of it is the main point. The virst verb, <span style="color:#008080">yddgtt</span>, is found in six other places, and rendered in all, except in Jeremiah 5:7, to cut one’s self; but its other meaning, as in Jeremiah 5:7, and evidently here is to troop or band together; and the noun <span style="color:#008080">dwdg</span>, which follows, commonly means a band or a troop. The participle <span style="color:#008080">µç</span>, in the next clause, can refer to nothing in the text but to “the daughter of a troop.” The obvious and natural rendering of the verse would be the following,— <span style="color:#000080">*Band thyself together, thou daughter of a band,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Laying against us a siege:—*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*With the rod shall they strike on the cheek*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*The judge of Israel.*</span> The daughter of a band or a troop means a military power, which collects bands or troops for warlike purposes. It is certainly more obvious to apply this to the Babylonian power than to Jerusalem, especially as the next line, “Laying against us a siege,” necessarily refers to the latter. “The judge” is, as Calvin seems to take it, a poetical singular for the plural. No particular person is meant, as *Newcome* and others seem to think, but judges in general.—*Ed.*

[^f86]: This does not follow; for to say that it was “not the least,” is not to deny that it was “small.” There is, in fact, no contradiction in the expressions. Matthew quotes literally neither the Hebrew nor the Septuagint version. The latter, in this case, agrees with the former. He gives the sense, but not the words, even in two instances besides this. Instead of “Ephratah,” he has, “in the land of Judah;” and instead of “Ruler,” he has, “Governor that shall rule,” or feed. The meaning in these three instances is the same, though the words are different. The place was, in former times, called Bethlehem-Judah, and also Ephratah. See Genesis 35:19; Judges 17:7; and Ruth 4:11. The attempt by a question to produce similarity of expressions in the second line, according to what is done by *Marckius* and *Newcome*, is by no means to be approved. The literal rendering is the following:— <span style="color:#000080">*And thou, Bethlehem Ephratah!*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Small to be among the thousands of Judah,—*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*From thee shall*</span> <span style="color:#000080">one</span> <span style="color:#000080">*to me come forth,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*To be a Ruler in Israel:*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And his going forth*</span> <span style="color:#000080">has been</span> <span style="color:#000080">*From of old, from the days of ages.*</span> The word for “going forth” is plural, which, as Calvin says, is sometimes used for the singular; but two MSS. Have it in the singular number, <span style="color:#008080">wtaxm</span>. The last line in the Septuagint is as follows,—<span style="color:#0000d4">ap</span> <span style="color:#0000d4">archv</span>, <span style="color:#0000d4">ex hmerwn aiwnov</span>. “In every age, from the foundation of the world, there has been some manifestation of the Messiah. He was the hope, as he was the salvation, of the world, from the promise to Adam in paradise, to his manifestation in the flesh *four thousand* years after.”—*Adam Clarke.—Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 266 -->

[^f87]: <span style="color:#008080">ˆkl</span>, *Grotius* renders it *certè—* surely: but *nevertheless,* as proposed by *Scott,* is the most suitable particle here. *Dathius* gives this paraphrase—*Verum quidem est—* True indeed it is.—*Ed.*

[^f88]: Until the time the begetting shall beget, (<span style="color:#008080">hdly hdlwy</span>) And the remnant of his brethren shall be converted Together with the children of Israel. *Newcome* gives this explanation of the verse,—”The sense is: God will not fully vindicate and exalt his people, till the Virgin-mother shall have brought forth her Son; and till Judah and Israel, and all the true sons of Abraham among their brethren, the Gentiles, be converted to Christianity.”—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 267 -->

[^f89]: By this arrangement of the sentence, *Calvin* evidently meant, that “his,” before “brethren,” refers to “Israel.” In the original, the latter clause is before the former, but in Hebrew, as well as in other languages, the antecedent sometimes comes after its pronoun.—*Ed.*

[^f90]: Most commentators differ from Calvin in their view of this verse, regarding it as a distinct prophecy of the Savior’s birth. There are difficulties on both sides: but taking the whole context, especially the following verse, we can hardly resist the conclusion, that Christ, born of a Virgin, is the subject. Indeed, the whole of this chapter, notwithstanding the reference to the Assyrian, is not capable of a satisfactory explanation, without applying what is said to Christ and his Church. Some things, no doubt, in the history of the Jews, may be alluded to, or incidentally mentioned; but the full accomplishment must be looked for in the new dispensation. And it is a splendid prophecy, in words often derived from customs and incidents among the Jews, of the birth of the Savior, and the character and extent, and blessedness of his kingdom, and the destruction of his enemies. *Newcome* and *Adam Clarke* propose to divide the chapter after the first line in verse 5, thinking that a new subject is there introduced: but evidently the same subject, the Gospel dispensation, is continued to the end of the chapter. The Assyrian, the especial enemy of the ancient Church, designates the enemies of the Christian Church in all ages. “As Sennacherib’s invasion,” says *Scott,* “was not repelled by the ruler or chieftains of Israel: nor did the Jews ever invade or waste the Assyrian dominions; it seems evident, that these expressions must be understood as mystically intending other enemies and persecutors of the Church, who should be of the same spirit with Sennacherib and the Assyrians.” *Henry*, who is much more learned critic and much profounder divine than what is commonly thought, agrees with *Scott,* and many others, in the interpretation of this chapter.—*Ed.*

[^f90]: “The Prophets prefaced their messages with, *Thus saith the Lord;* but Christ spoke not as a servant, but as a Son, *Verily, verily, I say unto you:* this was feeding in the majesty of the name of the Lord his God; all power was given him in heaven and earth, a power over all flesh, by the virtue of which he still rules in the majesty of the name of the Lord his God.”—*Henry.*

<!-- PAGE 268 -->

[^f91]: The order of the words in Hebrew is not strictly observed in this instance. There is here an example, not infrequent in the prophets, of the nominative case absolute,— <span style="color:#000080">*And he shall be*</span> <span style="color:#000080">our</span> <span style="color:#000080">*peace:*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*The Assyrian—when he shall come into our land,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And when he shall tread in our palaces,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*The raise shall we against him*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Seven shepherds and eight anointed men.*</span> <span style="color:#008080">µda ykysn</span>, literally anointed of men; but it is a phrase signifying men in authority, princes or sovereigns. <span style="color:#008080">µykysn</span> is rendered *dukes* in Joshua 13:21, and *princes* in Psalm 83:11, and Ezekiel 32:30. It is not necessary to say “eight princes of men,” but, “eight princes,” or “eight anointed men.”—*Ed.*

[^f92]: It is by no means a safe rule, to draw a conclusion from the spiritual government as to what a temporal government should be. The subjects are guided by very different principles; and the same sort of government will not suit countries under different degrees of civilization. To theorize on this subject, as on many others, leads often to wrong conclusions. An hereditary sovereignty may seem to trench on liberty; but our own country exhibits an example where both exist to an extent unknown in the present or in any former age. Under no democracy has liberty ever been so freely and so fully enjoyed as in this land, which has been so wonderfully favored by a kind and gracious Providence. We owe, perhaps, far more than we are aware to an hereditary sovereignty.—*Ed.*

[^f93]: <span style="color:#008080">hyjtpk</span>, in its openings or entrances: so most render the word. <span style="color:#0000d4">Entov</span> <span style="color:#0000d4">polwn authv</span>—within its gates.—*Symmachus. Marckius, Newcome,* and *Henderson,* agree with our version. Calvin has, in this instance, followed Kimchi and Aben-Ezra: but the affix <span style="color:#008080">h</span> prevents us from adopting this meaning; besides, the word itself is nowhere found in this sense. This verse is connected with the preceding, and ought to be separated from it only be a semicolon, and may be thus rendered:— <span style="color:#000080">*And they shall waste the land of the Assyrian by the sword,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And the land of Nimrod at its entrances:*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Thus shall he cause a deliverance from the Assyrian,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*When he shall come into our land,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And when he shall tread on our borders.—*</span><span style="color:#000080">Ed.</span>

<!-- PAGE 269 -->

[^f94]: There seems to be no necessity for this supposed inaccuracy in this comparison; it indeed changes the obvious meaning of the passage. The Jews are compared to the dew and rain, through which the grass grows; and then it is said, that the growth of the grass, not the dew or the rain, is not dependent on man, but on the dew or rain. The comparison is thus in every way suitable.—*Ed.*

[^f95]: We have the residue or remnant of Joseph in Amos 5:15,—the remnant of Israel in Micah 2:12,—and here in the following verse, the remnant of Jacob.—*Ed.*

[^f96]: “They shall be bold as a lion in witnessing against the corruptions of the times and places they live in, and strong as a lion in the strength of the Lord, to resist and overcome their spiritual enemies. *The weapons of their warfare* are *mighty through God, to the pulling down of strongholds,* 2 Corinthians 10:4,5. They shall have courage which all their adversaries shall not be able to resist, Luke 21:15.”—*Henry.*

[^f97]: As a curious instance of ingenuity and extravagance in allegorizing, practiced by some of the Fathers, *Jerome’s* interpretation of this verse may be mentioned: the *horses* were lascivious lusts; the *chariots,* sins joined together in which the wicked, as it were, ride and triumph; the *cities,* such as that built by Cain, not like the heavenly Jerusalem; and the *strongholds,* were riches and the pomps of the world, the eloquence of orators and the tenterhooks of dialecticians!—*Ed.*

[^f98]: From <span style="color:#008080">ãçk</span>. “In Arabic,” says Parkhurst, “the verb signifies to *discover, disclose, reveal,* and is always in the Hebrew Bible applied to some species of *conjuring.*” The Septuagint render the word here <span style="color:#0000d4">farmaka</span>, drugs or charms. They were enchanters or sorcerers, who applied drugs to magical purposes. See 2 Chronicles 33:6.—*Ed.*

[^f99]: The word here is <span style="color:#008080">µynnw[m</span>, from <span style="color:#008080">ˆn[</span>, a cloud. Parkhurst renders it *cloudmongers,* who looked upwards to the clouds either on the flight of birds, or on the stars, or on meteors, and thereby pretended to foretell future things. <span style="color:#0000d4">Apofqeggomenouv</span>—oraclers—*Sept. Theodoret* renders it <span style="color:#0000d4">manteiv</span>—soothsayers; and *Cyril* <span style="color:#0000d4">yeudomanteiv</span> —false prophets. Some derive it from <span style="color:#008080">hn[</span>, to answer; and others from <span style="color:#008080">ˆy[</span>, the eye; and hence, eyers or observers, either of times, or dreams, or of stars, or of birds.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 270 -->

[^f100]: “Many of them depended much upon the conduct and advice of their conjurors, diviners, and fortune-tellers, and these God will cut off, not only as weak things, and insufficient to relieve them, but as wicked things, and sufficient to ruin them.”—*Henry.*

[^f101]: <span style="color:#008080">twbxm</span>, rather pillars or columns than statues: <span style="color:#0000d4">tav sthlav</span> in the *Sept.* The pillar of stone which Jacob set up is called by this name, Genesis 28:18. They were commemorative pillars at which the Canaanites, and afterwards the Jews, offered idolatrous worship. There was a pillar of this kind in the house of Baal, 2 Kings 10:26, 27. They were not altars, though altars might have been reared by them, for both are mentioned together in Deuteronomy 12:3. The word is derived from <span style="color:#008080">bxy</span>, to set, to fix firmly. The noun is rendered by *Parkhurst, a standing pillar.—Ed.*

[^f102]: *Scott,* speaking of the latter part of this chapter, says, “The reformation of the Jews after their return from Babylon might be alluded to; but the purification of the Christian Church from all antichristian corruptions of faith and worship, and all idolatry and superstition, seems more immediately to be predicted.”—*Ed.*

[^f103]: *Newcome* renders the word <span style="color:#008080">˚yr[</span>, thine enemies, and not, thy cities, though he connects the verse differently,—more with the last than with the former portion of this,— <span style="color:#000080">*I will also destroy thine enemies:*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*15. And I will execute vengeance, in anger and in fury,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Upon the nations which have not hearkened*</span> <span style="color:#000080">unto me.</span>

[^f104]: *Henry* says, “Sin begets a controversy between God and man. The righteous God has an action against every sinner, an action of debt, an action of trespass, an action of slander.”

[^f105]: The verb is <span style="color:#008080">˚ytalh</span>, I have wearied, or caused thee to be weary. *Quo fatigavi te—*In what have I wearied thee? *Jun.* and *Trem.* <span style="color:#0000d4">Ti eluphsa</span> <span style="color:#0000d4">se</span>—how have I caused thee to grieve? *Sept. Quo labore te pressi—* with what labor have I oppressed thee? *Jerome.* This last contains the full meaning.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 271 -->

[^f106]: The complete sentence is, “from the house of servants,” or rather, slaves: for they were not properly what we call servants, but slaves, in Egypt. The Septuagint has <span style="color:#0000d4">ex oikou douleiav</span>—from the house of slavery. “The house of slaves,” is the version both of Newcome and of Henderson. They are the same words as we find in Exodus 20:2, rendered, “out of the house of bondage;” which ought to be translated slavery rather than bondage, if we depart from the literal rendering— the house of slaves.—*Ed.*

[^f107]: Various have been the ways to complete this evidently defective sentence; and there is no assistance from any MSS., or from the Septuagint. Shittim was in the land of Moab, and Gilgal was beyond Jordan, in the land of Canaan. *Grotius* and many others repeat the word “Remember,” and supply, “what I have done,” or, “what happened.” This is a sort of omission, which we can hardly think a writer would have made. It is far more probable that a word or words have been somehow left out: and the *Targum,* though generally no safe guide, has so given the passage as to countenance this conjecture. “Were not great things done for you,” is the supplement of the Targum. “And what I did,” seems to be the most natural addition: such words as <span style="color:#008080">ytyç[ hmw</span> appear to have been left out by transcribers. I would then render the verse thus:— <span style="color:#000080">*My people, remember, I pray,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*What did Balak, the king of Moab, consult,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And what did Balaam, the son of Beor, answer him,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*(And what I did) from Shittim even to Gilgal,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*That ye may know the faithful dealings of Jehovah.—*</span><span style="color:#000080">Ed.</span>

[^f107]: “His justice in destroying the Canaanites, his goodness in giving rest to his people Israel, and his faithfulness to his promises made unto the Fathers.”—*Henry.*

[^f108]: Literally, “the god of the height,” that is, of heaven, <span style="color:#008080">µwrm yhla</span>. See Psalm 68:18.

[^f109]: This clause is omitted in my Latin copy; and viewing it as an accidental omission, I have supplied it.—*Ed.*

[^f110]: The substance of the two lines is given here, not in their literal version. He evidently gives somewhat a different meaning from our translation; and probably the original admits better of the construction adopted here. If <span style="color:#008080">[çp</span>, a transgression or trespass, be taken for a trespass- offering, and <span style="color:#008080">hafj</span>, for a sin-offering, as it is often, then the rendering would be this,— <span style="color:#000080">*Shall I make my first-born my trespass-offering,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*The fruit of my loins a sin-offering for my soul?*</span> The verb <span style="color:#008080">ˆtn</span> is not only to give, to present, to offer, but also to make, to constitute, to appoint. But if the first be rendered transgression, the law of parallelism requires that the second should be rendered sin.— *Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 272 -->

[^f111]: The expression is remarkable—*to love mercy,* or benevolence, beneficence, or kindness; it is not only to show mercy or kindness, but to *love* it, so as to take pleasure and delight in it.*—Ed.*

[^f112]: The words are, <span style="color:#008080">˚yhlaAµ[ tkl [nxhw</span>. The verb <span style="color:#008080">[nx</span> occurs nowhere else but as a passive participle in Proverbs 11:2; but its meaning there is evident, for it is opposed to pride, <span style="color:#008080">ˆwdz</span>, which means a swelling pride, such as fills one with high notions of one’s self. Then the opposite of this is to be humble from a sense of one’s own emptiness. As it is here to the infinitive Hiphil, its literal meaning is what Calvin assigns to it—tohumble one’s self. And the best rendering of this line would be—”And to humble thyself to walk with God.” The Septuagint renders it <span style="color:#0000d4">etoimon enai</span>—to be ready; Theodotion, <span style="color:#0000d4">asfalizou</span>; Vulgate, *solicitum.* But these seem not to have understood the word. The *Welsh* version is exactly and literally the Hebrew—*Ac ymostwng I rodio gyda ‘th Dduw. Gostwng* is to humble, and by adding *ym,* and dropping the *g,* the verb has exactly the meaning of the Hiphil in Hebrew—to humble one’s self. They are, indeed, *some* verbs in *Welsh* which admit of all the modifications of the Hebrew verbs, being active, passive, causative, and reflective.—*Ed.*

[^f113]: And so *Newcome* renders it, and there are a few copies in favor of this reading, in which <span style="color:#008080">yary</span> is found: but a fact of this kind is not sufficient to make a change, except there will be other reasons. And then in the next line there is a change made, without the authority of one MS. Indeed these two lines are rendered as though the Archbishop had another text; and indeed it is another: his version is this,— <span style="color:#000080">*And*</span> <span style="color:#000080">there is</span> <span style="color:#000080">*a sound wisdom with them that fear his name:*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Hear, O ye tribes, him that testifieth.*</span> This version is partly derived from the Septuagint; which could not have been wholly followed, as it differs so widely from the Hebrew, and hardly presents any meaning. There is far more correspondence in the passage, as it is rendered in our version, and by *Calvin,* and also by *Henderson;* and the Hebrew is closely followed. *Drusius* and others agree with *Calvin,* that <span style="color:#008080">hyçwt</span>, which is rendered often “sound wisdom,” is to be taken here as concrete, signifying a wise man. <span style="color:#008080">hfm</span> is evidently the rod of correction, and is used in this sense in Isaiah 10:5, 24; and it is more consistent with the whole passage to consider <span style="color:#008080">hd[y</span> as a future, construed, as in the present tense, with an affix, from <span style="color:#008080">d[</span>, to testify, than from <span style="color:#008080">d[y</span> to appoint,—”Hear ye the rod,” the chastening rod, “and who testifies of it.” *Newcome* viewed it as being this verb; but he takes no notice of its affix <span style="color:#008080">h</span>, which refers to the rod, by which chastisement is signified.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 273 -->

[^f114]: One MS. Has <span style="color:#008080">çyh</span>, which no doubt is the true reading. The Septuagint has <span style="color:#0000d4">mhpur</span>, which seems to have no sense whatever. Many copies have <span style="color:#008080">çyah</span>, and this is the reading followed by *Junius* and *Tremelius,* and their version is this,— <span style="color:#000080">*Has any one still the house of a dishonest*</span> <span style="color:#000080">man?</span> <span style="color:#000080">*The treasures of dishonesty?*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And the small detestable ephah?—*</span><span style="color:#000080">Ed.</span>

[^f115]: Literally it is, “And the ephah of detestable scantiness?” *Marckius* renders the words, “Et ephah tenuitatis abominabilis?” *Henderson,* “And the accursed *scanty ephah?*”

[^f116]: <span style="color:#008080">hkzah</span>. It is not true what *Henderson* says, that the verb <span style="color:#008080">hkz</span> is not used transitively. See Psalm 73:13; Proverbs 20:9. *Jerome* renders the phrase, *numquid approbabo?* Our own version is no doubt correct.—*Ed.*

[^f117]: There is nothing in what goes before for which a reason is given here: hence this <span style="color:#008080">rça</span> cannot be rendered as here proposed. It is an instance of a peculiarity in Hebrew, when a double pronoun is used. Literally it is, “Which the rich men of hers;” the reference is to the city mentioned in verse 9. *Grotius, Newcome,* and *Henderson,* render the words thus, “Whose rich men,” etc. The *Welsh* is very nearly the same, which no more than the Hebrew can with propriety be literally rendered in English or in the learned languages,—*Yr hon y mae ei chyvoethogion yn llawn trais—*The which her rich men are full of violence. But this mode of speaking has a more distinct and fuller reference to what is gone before than the simple relative “whose:” the connection is made more evident.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 274 -->

[^f117]: *Newcome* renders this line differently,— <span style="color:#000080">*Wherefore I will begin go smite thee.*</span> Following a few MSS. And the Septuagint, he takes the verb here to be <span style="color:#008080">ytljh</span>, which means, to begin; but the rendering seems flat, and suits not the passage; and it is not true, for the Lord has often smitten them before. The verb is in the past tense, and this has created a difficulty. The verbs in the following verse, which is connected with this, are all in the future tense, referring to a coming judgment. To remove this difficulty I propose the following version,— <span style="color:#000080">*But even I,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">who</span> <span style="color:#000080">*have made*</span> <span style="color:#000080">thee</span> <span style="color:#000080">*to grieve by striking thee,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">Will</span> <span style="color:#000080">*make*</span> <span style="color:#000080">thee</span> <span style="color:#000080">*wholly desolate on account of thy sins:*</span> Then the threatened desolation is specified. The verb <span style="color:#008080">µmçh</span>, making desolate, is evidently a participle connected with <span style="color:#008080">yna</span> I, at the beginning of the verse, the rest being an intervening clause: and when a participle follows a nominative case, which often occurs in Hebrew, the auxiliary verb must be supplied in a translation, which in its tense must be regulated by the context, and here by the verse which follows. *Piscator* renders it *Desolabo,* and says, that it is an infinitive put for the future. Grief or sorrow had already been produced, but now entire desolation is threatened.—*Ed.*

[^f118]: *Newcome,* without the authority of a single MS., but following the Septuagint and *Houbigant,* has changed <span style="color:#008080">˚jçy</span> into <span style="color:#008080">˚çjy</span>, “it shall be dark.” Though the meaning of the passage is not thus materially affected, it is an alteration without sufficient reasons, there being no MS. In its favor, and no necessity arising from the passage itself: indeed, dejection or depression, or casting down, is more suitable to the context, and more emphatical.—*Ed.*

[^f119]: The verb is <span style="color:#008080">nst</span>, which *Henderson* considers to be in Hephil, the <span style="color:#008080">y</span> being left out, which is sometimes the case: with *Drusius* and others, he renders it, “remove,” that is not *goods,* as he says, but wives and children; for if any were for a time removed to a place of safety, they were afterwards to be given up to the sword. Several copies have <span style="color:#008080">ç</span> instead of <span style="color:#008080">s</span>, which makes it to be the verb <span style="color:#008080">gçn</span>, and this has the meaning of laying hold or apprehending. But either meaning will suit the context.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 275 -->

[^f120]: The verb, <span style="color:#008080">rmtçy</span>, is in the singular, and is followed by its nominative case, which is in the plural number. Grammarians are at a loss to account for this, and hence propose several modes of construction. But it is evidently an anomalous idiom, somewhat similar to that in Greek, when plural neuters take a verb in the singular number. As it has been already observed, such a construction as we find here, is very common in the *Welsh* language. The verb is in Hithpael, the reflective mood, the <span style="color:#008080">t</span>, as often the case, changing place with the first letter of the verb. It is not always that this mood is reflective, but is sometimes passive, as we find to be the case with <span style="color:#008080">rts</span>, in Isaiah 29:14, and <span style="color:#008080">rb[</span>, in Deuteronomy 3:26. And so here it does not retain its reflective meaning. But it may be, that intensity, diligence, or earnestness, is intended to be conveyed; that is, that the statutes of Omri were diligently and carefully observed.—*Ed.*

[^f121]: There is another view mentioned by *Drusius,—* that is, the *reproach* which God had previously denounced on his people, in case they sinned and continued in their perverseness. Reproach in this sense would mean punishment.—*Ed.*

[^f122]: *Newcome* renders the verse somewhat different, and makes the comparison more clear,— <span style="color:#000080">*“Woe is me! For I am become*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*As the gatherers of late figs,\|*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*As the gleaners of the vintage:*</span> <span style="color:#000080">There is</span> <span style="color:#000080">*no cluster to eat;*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*My soul desireth the first ripe fig.”*</span> Substantially the same is the version of *Dathius* and of *Henderson.* “Late figs” is not strictly the meaning of <span style="color:#008080">≈yq</span>, which id properly summer or summer-fruit; yet, as the early or first ripe fig is mentioned in the last line, which forms a contrast with this, what is meant, no doubt, is the late figs. Then the word for “gleaners,” <span style="color:#008080">tll[</span>, is properly, gleanings; but here it is evidently to be taken as a concrete, gleaners, to correspond with gatherers, though *Newcome* considers the women-gleaners to be intended. The four last lines form a parallelism, in which the first and the early fig,—the vintage and the cluster.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 276 -->

[^f123]: *Justus,* rendered in the text *humanus,* vel, *mansuetus.* The Hebrew is <span style="color:#008080">rysj</span>, rendered by the Septuagint “<span style="color:#0000d4">eusbhv</span>—godly, pious,”—by *Marckius,* “benignus—kind, benignant,” —by *Newcome,* “the good man,”—and by *Henderson,* “the pious.” It is sometimes rendered holy; but its meaning is, kind, benevolent, merciful, actively good, beneficent. In Psalm 12:1, it is rendered “godly,” and in Isaiah 57:1, “merciful.”—*Ed.*

[^f124]: *Rectus,* <span style="color:#008080">rçy</span>, rendered by the Septuagint, “<span style="color:#0000d4">katorqwn</span>—one going straight to an object,”—by *Newcome* and *Henderson,* “upright.” It is one who proceeds in a straight course according to the rule of the law, without making any windings or turning aside into any devious path.— *Ed.*

[^f125]: This clause, though the general sense is allowed by most to be the same, is yet variously rendered. *Drusius* says, “Locus hic diu me multumque torsit.” The original is,— <span style="color:#008080">byfyhl µypk [rh l[</span> The most satisfactory rendering is that which is offered by *Marckius,* which is this,— <span style="color:#000080">*Propter malefaciendum volae pro benefaciendum,—*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*For doing evil [*</span><span style="color:#000080">are</span> <span style="color:#000080">*τηειρ hands instead of doing good.*</span> Rabbi *Jonathan,* as quoted by *Marckius,* gives substantially the same rendering, though not literally,— <span style="color:#000080">*Malum faciunt manibus suis, et non bonum faciunt,—*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Evil they do with their hands, and they do no good.*</span> Our version is that of *Junius* and *Tremelius*, and is substantially followed by *Newcome;* and *Henderson’s* version is, — <span style="color:#000080">*For evil their hands are well prepared;*</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">which is nearly that of the Septuagint,—</p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#0000d4">Epi to kakon tav ceirav autwn etoimazousi</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 277 -->

But the following would be as literal a translation as that of *Marckius,*—

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*For doing evil*</span> <span style="color:#000080">are</span> <span style="color:#000080">*their hands, to do*</span> <span style="color:#000080">it</span> <span style="color:#000080">*thoroughly.*</span></p>

The last verb means not only to do good, but also to make a thing good or complete, fully to execute it.—*Ed.*

[^f126]: The whole verse may be rendered thus,— <span style="color:#000080">*For doing evil*</span> <span style="color:#000080">are</span> <span style="color:#000080">*their hands, to execute it fully:*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*The prince asks, and the judge*</span> <span style="color:#000080">also,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*for reward;*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*When the great man speaks of oppression,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">That</span> <span style="color:#000080">*it*</span> <span style="color:#000080">is</span> <span style="color:#000080">*his desire, then they contrive it together,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">or, literally,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*entwine it.*</span> To render <span style="color:#008080">awh wçpn twh</span>, “the wickedness of the soul,” as *Newcome* does, is to leave out wholly the last word; and *Henderson* does the same. *Piscator* gives the form of the words, “aerumnam, quam expetit—the mischief, which he desires.” The two last words literally are, “his desire it *is.*”—*Ed.*

[^f127]: It is better, as it is done here, to take the words simply as they are, and not to make superlatives of them: nor is there any change necessary in the second line as proposed by *Dathius, Newcome,* and others, by taking the <span style="color:#008080">m</span> from one word and attaching it to another. There is no MS. In its favor, and it is done only on the authority of the Targum. The two lines are these,— <span style="color:#000080">*Their good*</span> <span style="color:#000080">man</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is like a brier,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*The upright worse than a thorny hedge.*</span> The preposition <span style="color:#008080">m</span> is often rendered “rather than;” but it may, in many places, be rendered “better than,” or “worse than,” according to the import of the passage.—*Ed.*

[^f128]: See *Ov. Met.* Lib. I. 144-148.

[^f129]: *Ne fidatis amico:* it is rather, Believe not in a friend, that is, in what he says, <span style="color:#008080">[rb wnymatAla</span>. The next expression is that which signifies reliance, trust or confidence. <span style="color:#008080">ãwla</span>, is a leader; <span style="color:#0000d4">hgoumenov</span> in Sept., one who leads the way. *Diodati* gives its true meaning,—”A conductor, the most trusty friend, who is one’s usual counselor in every difficulty and perplexity.” *Jerome* refers to scriptural instances as to the persons here mentioned: the *friend,* Ahitophel and Judas,—the *counselor,* Abimelek, who was made king by the men of Sichem, and oppressed them,—*domestics,* Absalom and the wives of Esau. The word used for “dishonoring” is very strong; <span style="color:#008080">lbnm</span>, one who counts a thing worthless or abominable; it means not only to dishonor, but to regard with disdain and contempt. “The contempt and violation of the laws of domestic duties,” *Henry* justly observes, “are a sad symptom of an universal corruption of manners. Those are never likely to come to good who are undutiful to their parents, and study to be provoking to them and cross them.”—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 278 -->

[^f130]: This is not exactly the Hebrew. The verb for rising, as well as that for falling, is in the past tense. The verse, literally rendered, is the following:— <span style="color:#000080">*Rejoice not, my enemy, on my account;*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Though I have fallen, I have risen;*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Though I shall sit in darkness,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Jehovah*</span> <span style="color:#000080">will be</span> <span style="color:#000080">*a light to me.*</span> There are no copies which give a different reading as to the verb “I have risen.” *Newcome* follows the Septuagint, and thinks that a conversive <span style="color:#008080">w</span> is left out. It ought rather perhaps to be considered as the language of faith, realizing the event before it arrived. The fall and “the darkness” refer no doubt to the outward calamities of the Church, its troubles and afflictions.—*Ed.*

[^f131]: *Iram,* <span style="color:#008080">ã[z</span>, which means a stormy anger or displeasure, which agitates and raises tempests, and such were the calamities which came on the Jewish nation.

[^f132]: “I shall see the equity of his proceedings concerning me, and the performance of his promises to me.”—*Henry.*

[^f133]: It is somewhat singular that *Newcome* renders the first “fenced” and the second “Egypt:” but Henderson renders both “Egypt.” It is not the common name for Egypt, which is <span style="color:#008080">µyrxm</span>; the places referred to, 2 Kings 19:24, and Isaiah 19:6, do not justify this application. The word “day” in three instances is here without a preposition: it may therefore be regarded as the nominative absolute, or the verb, is nigh, or approaches, as Jerome proposes, is understood. I would give this version of the two verses,— <span style="color:#000080">*11. The day for building thy walls!*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*That day! Removed far shall be the decree:*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*12. That day! Even to thee shall they come,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*From Assyria and cities of fortress,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And from the fortress even to the river,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And*</span> <span style="color:#000080">from</span> <span style="color:#000080">*sea to sea, and*</span> <span style="color:#000080">from</span> <span style="color:#000080">*mountain to mountain,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">or, word for word,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*And from the fortress even to the river and the sea,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*From the sea and the mountain of the mountain.*</span> The last expression seems to mean, “every mountain.”—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 279 -->

[^f134]: The copulative <span style="color:#008080">w</span>, rendered *et, and,* in the text, is not noticed here. *Newcome* renders it *For,* connecting this with the former verse, and applying it to heathen lands. But *Dathius* and *Henderson* render it, as an adversative, *But, Nevertheless,* and consider, with Calvin, that the land of Israel is here meant.—*Ed.*

[^f135]: “The crook signifies God’s peculiar care for his people.”—*Grotius.*

[^f136]: “He compares the elect people,” says *Marckius,* “to a flock of sheep, because they resemble them in weakness, in innocency, in meekness, in usefulness, in fruitfulness, and in close union. See Psalm 95:7; Isaiah 40:11; Ezekiel 34:12; Zechariah 9:16, 10:3; John 10:16, etc.” “They are thy sheep, thy peculiar property, who hear thee, who need thy guidance and feeding, for they are weak and helpless, and liable to go astray without the preserving care of their Shepherd.”—*Cocceius.*

[^f137]: These two lines are better arranged by *Newcome,* and the necessity of a preposition understood is obviated, while the original is more strictly rendered,— <span style="color:#000080">*In the midst of Carmel let them feed,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*In Bashan and Gilead, as in the days of old.*</span> It is also better to render “feed” as a prayer than in the future tense, to correspond in tenor with the beginning of the verse. *Henderson* connects “Carmel” with the former line, and thinks that “dwelling alone in the wood” refers to the condition of the Jews when restored, and quotes the prophecy of Balaam in Numbers 23:9. But this seems to be a far-fetched exposition; and the word “wood,” which means generally a dreary place, renders it wholly inadmissible. A state of destitution and misery is evidently intended. “They were now,” says *Henry,* “a desolate people; they were in the land of their captivity as sheep in a forest, in danger of being lost and made a prey to the beasts of the forest.”—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 280 -->

[^f138]: “The Prophet prayed that God would feed them, and do kind things for them; but God answers, that he will show them marvelous things, will outdo their hopes and expectations.—Their deliverance from Babylon shall be a work of wonder and grace, not inferior to their deliverance out of Egypt, nay, it shall eclipse the luster of that, Jeremiah 16:14, 15.—God’s former favors to his Church are patterns of future favors, and shall again be copied out as there is occasion.”—*Henry.*

[^f139]: “They shall be ashamed of the strength in which they trusted,”— *Drusius;* or as *Grotius* says, “of all their strength which had been so suddenly destroyed;” or, as another author says, “of all their strength when found ineffectual for the purpose of destroying the people of God.”—*Ed.*

[^f140]: “Malice,” says *Jerome,* “not only blinds the eyes, but also deafens the ears.”—*Ed.*

[^f141]: *Dathius* renders these two lines differently, “Jovam Deum nostrum timebunt eumque reverebuntur—Jehovah our God they shall fear, and him will they reverence.” But this is neither consistent with the passage, nor with the form in which the words appear. <span style="color:#008080">djp</span> is not commonly, if ever, a transitive verb, and to dread, or to be afraid, and not to fear, is its usual meaning: and <span style="color:#008080">ary</span>, when it means the fear of reverence, is generally construed without a preposition, and with <span style="color:#008080">ta</span> before Jehovah. The literal rendering is no doubt that which is given by Calvin. The distich is capable of being rendered in *Welsh* exactly as in Hebrew, in the same form and with the same prepositions; and, when thus rendered, the meaning is what is give here,— <span style="color:#000080">*Oherwydd Jehova ein Duw ur arswydant,—*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ac ovnant rhagddot.*</span> To fear because of thee, and to fear thee, are two distinct things. You will have the first form in Joshua 10:8; 11:6; and the second in Deuteronomy 31:12. The first refers to the fear of the Canaanites, the dread of their power; the second, to the fear of Jehovah.—*Ed.*

<!-- PAGE 281 -->

[^f142]: *Cocceius* calls this verse the *doxology* of the Church. *Jerome* renders the first words,—*Quis Deus similis tui?* Which is literally the original, <span style="color:#008080">laAym ˚wmk</span>—What God like thee? That is, Jehovah, mentioned in the preceding verse. <span style="color:#0000d4">Tiv Qeov wsper su</span>; *Sept.* Thinking that the verse will admit of somewhat different arrangement, and of a more literal rendering, I give the following,— <span style="color:#000080">*What God*</span> <span style="color:#000080">is</span> <span style="color:#000080">*like thee?*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Taking away iniquity, and passing over transgression!—*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Against the remnant of his heritage,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*He retaineth not for ever his anger,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*For a lover of mercy*</span> <span style="color:#000080">is</span> <span style="color:#000080">*He.*</span> There is a transition after the second line from the second to the third person; hence I make here the division. Besides, the construction of the third with the second has ever been found awkward by all the critics; but its construction with the fourth verse is perfectly natural. <span style="color:#008080">açn</span> “taking away,” or removing, conveys the idea of a burden being lifted up and removed. “Passing over” seems to allude to the angel passing over the houses of the Israelites in Egypt. “Iniquity,” <span style="color:#008080">ˆw[</span>, is guilt and inward depravity; and “Transgression,” <span style="color:#008080">[çp</span>, is an overt act of sin, an outward violation of the law. “A lover,” <span style="color:#008080">≈pj</span>; the Septuagint renders it, <span style="color:#0000d4">qelhthv eleouv</span>—the wisher of mercy; which is too weak, for the word means delight. It may be rendered, “For one who delights in mercy *is* He.” The *Targum* reads, *Amat enim benefacere—*for he loves to do good.—*Ed.*

[^f143]: *Grotius, Dathius,* and *Henderson,* consider that this verb, placed before another, without a conjunction, expresses only a reiteration; and they render it adverbially, “again.” But, in this place, it would be better to give it its proper meaning; for as God is said to depart from his people, Hosea 9:12, so he may be said also to return. The Septuagint renders it <span style="color:#0000d4">epistreyei</span>—He will return. *Drusius* reads, *convertetur,* scil. Ab ira sua—*He will turn,* that is, from his anger. *Newcome’s* version is, “He will turn again.”—*Ed.*

[^f144]: There is a mistake as to this verb; it is the *second* person, as are all the verbs which follow. The Prophet resumes here his address to God, which he commenced in the two first lines of the last verse. To show the difference between what he speaks *to* and what he speaks *of* God, the whole passage shall be here given,— <span style="color:#000080">*18. What God*</span> <span style="color:#000080">is</span> <span style="color:#000080">*like thee!*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Taking away iniquity, and passing over transgression!*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Against the remnant of his heritage*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*He retains not forever his anger;*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*For a lover of mercy*</span> <span style="color:#000080">is</span> <span style="color:#000080">*He;*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*19. He will return, he will pity us,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*He will subdue or iniquities:—*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Yea, thou wilt cast into the depths of the sea all their sins;*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Thou wilt show faithfulness to Jacob, mercy to Abraham,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Which thou swarest to our fathers in the days of old.*</span> “Pity,” <span style="color:#008080">µjfr</span>, is tender compassion; the noun in the plural number is used to designate the bowels. “Subdue,” or trample under foot, is rendered “cover” by *Newcome,* on the ground of this being the meaning of <span style="color:#008080">çbk</span> in Chaldee. This wholly destroys the striking character of the passage. Our sins are here represented as our enemies; God subdues them; and then in the next line the simile is continued, they are to be drowned like Pharaoh and his hosts in the depths of the sea. *Henderson’s* remarks on this point are very excellent. “There is no ground,” he says, “for rejecting the radical idea of *trampling under foot as enemies.* Sin must ever be regarded as hostile to man. It is not only contrary to his interests, but it powerfully opposes and combats the moral principles of his nature, and the higher principles implanted by grace; and but for the counteracting energy of divine influence, must prove victorious. Without the subjugation of evil propensities, pardon would not be a blessing.”—*Ed.*
